TL;DR: FOORT is a flexible, open-source C++ ray-tracing code for imaging black holes and compact objects in arbitrary spacetimes—including Kerr, naked singularities, fuzzballs, boson stars, and numerical metrics—featuring horizon-agnostic geodesic integration, diagnostic tracking (e.g., equatorial crossings, closest approach), adaptive mesh refinement for photon rings, and Python-based post-processing for images and visibilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a lightweight, general-purpose, and extensible ray-tracing framework capable of handling diverse spacetime geometries—including those without Killing symmetries or analytical conserved quantities—thereby enabling robust imaging studies of non-Kerr compact objects and numerical relativity metrics.

Method: FOORT integrates null geodesics numerically from the camera plane using a generic 4D metric input; it avoids reliance on conserved quantities by initializing geodesics directly in coordinate space; supports adaptive mesh refinement based on user-defined diagnostics; implements OpenMP parallelization; and interfaces with Python tools for image rendering and interferometric visibility computation.

Result: Validated against analytic Schwarzschild and Kerr results; successfully applied to non-Kerr metrics (e.g., boson stars, fuzzballs); demonstrates efficient scaling with OpenMP threads; produces high-resolution images resolving higher-order photon rings via adaptive diagnostics-driven refinement.

Conclusion: FOORT fills a gap in the computational astrophysics toolkit by offering a simple, modular, and physics-agnostic ray tracer that balances flexibility, accuracy, and usability—making it suitable for both theoretical explorations of exotic compact objects and observational modeling for EHT-like interferometry.

Abstract: We present FOORT (Flexible Object-Oriented Ray Tracing), an open C++ code for ray-tracing images of black holes and other compact objects in arbitrary spacetimes. FOORT is designed to offer flexibility and can handle horizons, naked singularities and numerical metrics, allowing it to ray-trace numerous different metrics including Kerr and other black holes, as well as horizonless objects such as fuzzballs and boson stars. New metrics, analytical or numerical, can be added with minimal effort, and geodesics are initialized at the camera without reference to conserved quantities, so no special structure of the metric is required. Besides the intensity observed from a geometrically and optically thin equatorial disk, FOORT is set up to track a number of diagnostics along each geodesic, such as the number of equatorial crossings and the radius of closest approach. Its adaptive mesh can refine the image on any of these diagnostics, efficiently resolving narrow features such as higher-order photon rings. Accompanying Python tools convert the output into images and interferometric visibility amplitudes. We describe the main features of the code, validate it against analytic results in the Schwarzschild and Kerr spacetimes, demonstrate it on several non-Kerr metrics, and characterize its scaling with the number of OpenMP threads. FOORT is lightweight, requiring only C++17 and OpenMP, and has already been used in a number of studies of black hole and compact-object imaging. The code is publicly available in a GitHub repository.

</details>


### [27] [Maximal force bounds in general relativity and beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15405)
*Sanhitaa Guha*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper derives upper bounds on the maximal force for stable, horizonless, compact objects in various gravitational theories—including d-dimensional GR, braneworld, pure Lovelock, and Einstein-Gauss-Bonnet gravity—using light-ring absence as a stability criterion and anisotropic matter models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize and test the Gibbons–Schiller maximal force conjecture beyond 4D general relativity, examining how dimensional and higher-curvature modifications affect force bounds for stable horizonless compact objects.

Method: Analyzes static, spherically symmetric, anisotropic compact objects; employs photon ring (light ring) absence as a stability condition; derives force bounds via gravitational field equations in d-dimensional and modified gravity frameworks (braneworld, pure Lovelock, Einstein-Gauss-Bonnet).

Result: Obtains dimension- and theory-dependent upper bounds on gravitational force for stable horizonless objects; shows bounds deviate from c⁴/G in higher dimensions and with higher-curvature corrections; quantifies dependence on radial/tangential pressure anisotropy.

Conclusion: The maximal force bound is not universal across gravitational theories or dimensions; stability constraints from light-ring absence yield theory-specific force ceilings, challenging the strict universality of the c⁴/G conjecture.

Abstract: Gibbons and Schiller proposed the maximal force conjecture in general relativity, according to which the maximum force is bounded by $c^4/G$. In this work, we derive upper bounds on the force for stable, self-gravitating, spatially regular and horizonless compact objects in $d$-dimensional general relativity, braneworld gravity, pure Lovelock gravity and Einstein-Gauss-Bonnet gravity. Our analysis uses the connection between photon-ring structure and the stability of horizonless compact objects, adopting the absence of light rings as a criterion for stability. We investigate how the resulting force bounds depend on the spacetime dimension and on modifications to gravitational dynamics arising from extra dimensions and higher curvature corrections. We study how the bounds for such systems change under alternative higher curvature theories of gravity or in higher dimensions. Throughout this work, we consider a static and spherically symmetric spacetime sourced by anisotropic matter for which the radial and tangential pressures may differ.

</details>


### [28] [Exact model of a gravitational wave and electromagnetic radiation in DHOST gravity theory: wave solutions and testable effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15414)
*Konstantin E. Osetrin*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper analytically investigates the coupled dynamics of gravitational waves and high-frequency electromagnetic fields in both General Relativity and DHOST gravity, confirming gravitationally induced waveguiding and deriving a scalar frequency drift effect that observationally distinguishes GR from DHOST Ia despite identical metric forms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To non-perturbatively test how gravitational waves influence electromagnetic propagation in modified gravity (specifically DHOST Ia), and to identify observable signatures that break degeneracy between theories with identical metrics.

Method: Uses an exact gravitational-wave spacetime in isotropic (null) wave coordinates to analytically integrate Maxwell's equations in both GR and DHOST class Ia; leverages the algebraic structure of DHOST constraint equations to derive modifications to EM wave parameters.

Result: Exact confirmation that GWs act as waveguides for co-directional EM radiation and generate an effective pure-radiation source; derivation of scalar-induced electromagnetic frequency drift—a novel, observationally testable discriminant between GR and DHOST Ia.

Conclusion: The scalar degree of freedom in DHOST Ia gravity alters electromagnetic wave propagation even when the metric matches GR's, yielding a measurable frequency drift effect—providing a clean, metric-degeneracy-breaking observational probe of beyond-GR physics.

Abstract: Within an analytical non-perturbative framework based on a single exact model, the coupled dynamics of gravitational waves and high-frequency electromagnetic fields is investigated in General Relativity and DHOST gravity. Using an exact gravitational-wave spacetime in an isotropic (null) wave coordinate system, Maxwell's equations are integrated analytically. This analysis provides an exact non-perturbative confirmation from the full field equations that a gravitational wave acts as a waveguide, forming a propagation channel for electromagnetic radiation co-directional with the gravitational wave and generating an effective pure-radiation source. Applying this exact electrodynamic solution to the nonlinear field equations of DHOST class~Ia gravity reveals that the algebraic structure of the DHOST constraint equations enforces a modification of the electromagnetic-wave parameters. Based on this mechanism, an observationally testable effect of scalar frequency drift is derived, providing a direct way to discriminate between General Relativity and modified gravity theories even when their metric tensors share identical functional forms.

</details>


### [29] [Exact transformation-optics representation of the Ellis wormhole and limits of finite-device scattering emulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15662)
*Abdullah Guvendi,Claudio Furtado,Omar Mustafa*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the electromagnetic response of the ultrastatic Ellis wormhole, derives its exact isotropic material representation via Plebański correspondence, proves a fundamental lower bound on reflection mismatch for lossless finite single-port devices, and provides rigorous multipole scattering scalings and constitutive error bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine which electromagnetic features of the ideal two-ended Ellis wormhole geometry can be physically realized by finite, passive, lossless media — particularly addressing the fundamental limits imposed by causality, topology, and energy conservation.

Method: Uses global isotropic coordinate transformation and Plebański correspondence to obtain exact impedance-matched continuum constitutive parameters; constructs radial vector-Maxwell operator; analyzes spectral properties and scattering via Jost solutions and Wronskians; applies complex analysis (reverse triangle inequality) to derive reflection mismatch bound; computes convergent multipole expansions; derives exact finite-band constitutive-error formula for calibrated passive Lorentz model.

Result: Proves |R_id| < 1 for all real frequencies in the ideal wormhole, implying strict impossibility of perfect reflection matching by any lossless finite single-port device; establishes lower bound on complex reflection-amplitude mismatch; derives low-frequency transmission scaling |T_L|² ∝ (ωa/c)^(4L+2); shows transmission cross section → πa² and total scattering cross section exhibits logarithmic forward divergence; provides exact finite-band constitutive-error formula for passive Lorentz model.

Conclusion: The ideal Ellis wormhole’s electromagnetic signature — especially its nonzero transmission across asymptotic ends — is topologically and causally forbidden for any finite, lossless, single-port realization; this yields a rigorous no-go theorem with quantitative bounds, distinguishing constitutive approximation from functional (scattering) equivalence.

Abstract: We derive a global isotropic representation of the ultrastatic Ellis wormhole and establish which features of its electromagnetic response can, and cannot, be reproduced by a finite device. An orientation-preserving coordinate map sends the signed proper-radial line to a punctured Euclidean chart with refractive index $n(\rho)=1+a^2/(4\rho^2)$. Through the Plebański correspondence, this gives the exact impedance-matched continuum medium $\varepsilon_r=\mu_r=n(\rho)\,\mathbb{1}$ and its full vector-Maxwell operator. For the ideal two-ended geometry, we derive the electromagnetic radial operator, establish its spectral properties, and express its scattering amplitudes through Jost solutions and Wronskians. Nonzero transmission into the second asymptotic end implies $|R_{\rm id}|<1$ at every real frequency, whereas a regular, lossless finite one-port has unit-modulus reflection. The reverse triangle inequality therefore yields a strictly positive lower bound on the complex reflection-amplitude mismatch. This theorem applies to the stated lossless single-port class and is not extended to lossy or multiport realizations. We compute converged multipole scattering data and establish the low-frequency threshold scaling $|T_L|^2\propto(\omega a/c)^{4L+2}$, with coefficients independently validated for $L=1,2,3,4$. The transmission cross section tends to $\pi a^2$, while the ideal profile's total scattering cross section has a logarithmic forward divergence. We also construct a smooth finite-shell target and derive an exact finite-band constitutive-error formula for a calibrated passive Lorentz model. The constitutive benchmark is kept distinct from scattering equivalence throughout.

</details>


### [30] [Highly spinning binary black holes: promise and pitfalls for parameterized tests of general relativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15721)
*Soumen Roy,Maria Haney,Justin Janquart*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper investigates how highly spinning, mass-asymmetric binary black hole mergers—probed via gravitational waves—can enhance tests of general relativity (GR) at higher post-Newtonian (PN) orders, but finds that waveform model inaccuracies (e.g., in IMRPhenomXPHM, IMRPhenomXPNR, SEOBNRv5PHM) induce systematic biases that mimic GR violations in parameterized tests (TIGER), especially for loud signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve constraints on higher-post-Newtonian deviations from general relativity using highly spinning, mass-asymmetric binary black hole inspirals—systems where strong-field, dynamical gravity is most pronounced and current PN modeling is least constrained.

Method: Uses numerical relativity (NR) waveforms from the SXS catalog; infers source parameters with three state-of-the-art phenomenological and effective-one-body waveform models (IMRPhenomXPHM-SpinTaylor, IMRPhenomXPNR, SEOBNRv5PHM); applies the TIGER framework—a parameterized test of GR based on IMRPhenomXPHM—to quantify apparent deviations; quantifies model-dependent biases and their impact on inferred GR violation significance.

Result: Waveform-model-dependent biases in mass, spin magnitude, and spin orientation are observed; these biases translate into spurious, statistically significant apparent deviations from GR in TIGER analyses—particularly for high-SNR signals—despite the injected signals being fully consistent with GR.

Conclusion: Highly spinning binaries are excellent probes of higher-PN physics, but current waveform systematics dominate over theoretical uncertainties in parameterized GR tests; thus, reducing waveform modeling errors is essential before claiming evidence for new physics from such tests.

Abstract: Highly spinning binary black holes exhibit complex inspiral dynamics and offer a powerful opportunity to probe gravity in a stronger-field and more dynamical regime than non-spinning binaries. Gravitational-wave signals from such systems enable stringent parameterized tests of deviations from the source dynamics predicted by general relativity. We show that highly spinning, mass-asymmetric binaries can provide enhanced sensitivity to deviations entering at higher post-Newtonian orders, which remain comparatively poorly constrained. Using NR simulations from the SXS catalog, we first recover the injected signals within general relativity using $\texttt{IMRPhenomXPHM-SpinTaylor}$, $\texttt{IMRPhenomXPNR}$, and $\texttt{SEOBNRv5PHM}$, finding model-dependent biases in the inferred masses, spins, and spin orientations. We then analyze the same signals with the $\texttt{IMRPhenomXPHM}$-based inspiral parameterized test of general relativity framework, TIGER, and find that these waveform-systematic biases can lead to apparent deviations from general relativity, which become significant for loud signals. Our results highlight that highly spinning binaries offer powerful tests of higher-order post-Newtonian deviations, but waveform inaccuracies can lead to false signs of new physics.

</details>


### [31] [Eccentricity detectability in Extreme Mass-Ratio Inspirals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15736)
*Maria Gonçalves Salgado,Lorenzo Speri,Elena M. Rossi*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the trade-off between eccentricity-driven distinguishability and harmonic power loss in detecting extreme mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) with LISA, finding that eccentricity becomes detectable above ~0.05 but single-harmonic searches degrade beyond ~0.1; including sufficient harmonics recovers SNR but increases statistical calibration cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how orbital eccentricity affects EMRI detectability in LISA — balancing improved distinguishability from circular templates against signal power dilution across harmonics in semi-coherent searches.

Method: Semi-coherent detection framework applied to EMRIs with fixed total SNR = 50; evaluated eccentricity threshold for distinguishability from zero, harmonic power distribution, SNR recovery thresholds, and statistical cost of multi-harmonic calibration; applied to two astrophysical EMRI population models.

Result: Eccentricity is distinguishable from zero for e_f ≳ 0.05; single-harmonic sensitivity declines beyond e_f ≈ 0.1; noise-free recovery of ≥50% total SNR requires sufficient harmonics, but introduces statistical freedom in threshold calibration.

Conclusion: The interplay among eccentricity-driven distinguishability, harmonic power loss, and statistical cost of multi-harmonic searches must be carefully balanced in designing future EMRI search pipelines for LISA.

Abstract: Extreme mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) consist of a stellar-mass compact object orbiting a massive black hole and are among the primary targets for the future space-based gravitational-wave detector LISA. Their orbital eccentricity carries valuable information about their astrophysical origins, making its detection particularly important. However, increasing eccentricity has competing effects on detectability: it makes eccentric waveforms easier to distinguish from circular ones, but also redistributes signal power across multiple harmonics, reducing the power captured by single-harmonic searches. In this work, we quantify this trade-off within a semi-coherent framework for a source with fixed total SNR of $50$. Eccentricity becomes distinguishable from zero for final eccentricities $e_f \gtrsim 0.05$, while single-harmonic sensitivity declines beyond $e_f \simeq 0.1$. In noise-free analyses, including enough harmonics to recover at least $50\%$ of the total SNR avoids this degradation, but adds statistical freedom when calibrating detection thresholds. We apply the framework to two astrophysical EMRI populations, showing how these competing effects interact with their eccentricity distributions under simplified assumptions. The balance among eccentricity-driven distinguishability, harmonic power loss, and the statistical cost of additional harmonics is an important consideration for future EMRI search developments.

</details>


### [32] [Population inference of eccentric binary black holes using asymptotic constants of motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15738)
*Chiara Anselmo,Giulia Fumagalli,Davide Gerosa*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel approach to gravitational-wave population inference for eccentric compact binaries by using the Newtonian orbital angular momentum— an asymptotic constant of motion—as a reference parameter instead of eccentricity, which diverges in the zero-frequency limit. The method enables consistent, physically grounded population modeling and allows post-inference propagation to any observational frequency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard population analyses use a fixed reference frequency, leading to inconsistent evolutionary stages across sources and complicating astrophysical interpretation; the zero-frequency (past-time infinity) limit offers a mathematically well-defined, uniform reference, but eccentricity is unsuitable there as it approaches unity for all systems, whereas Newtonian orbital angular momentum remains finite and constant.

Method: Reformulates eccentric binary population inference by replacing eccentricity with Newtonian orbital angular momentum as the primary eccentricity-related population parameter—leveraging its status as an asymptotic constant of motion in the relativistic two-body problem—and applies this framework to simulated gravitational-wave data using Bayesian hierarchical inference.

Result: Demonstrates successful eccentric population inference using only asymptotic constants of motion (specifically orbital angular momentum); shows that inferred posteriors can be reliably propagated to arbitrary frequencies for comparison with astrophysical models.

Conclusion: Using orbital angular momentum as a zero-frequency reference enables consistent, interpretable, and astrophysically flexible eccentric population inference—constituting the first such implementation and paving the way for robust tests of formation channels using gravitational-wave data.

Abstract: In standard gravitational-wave population analyses of compact binaries, source parameters are defined at a reference frequency. For quantities that evolve during the inspiral, this means constructing a population of systems at different evolutionary stages, which complicates the astrophysical interpretation of the results. The past-time infinity, or zero-frequency, limit provides a mathematically well-defined reference where all sources enter the population fit on equal footing. This approach was first proposed for spin tilts, which evolve due to spin precession but converge to constant values as binaries are back-propagated. The orbital eccentricity also evolves throughout the inspiral and approaches unity in the zero-frequency limit for all binaries, making it unsuitable as a common reference point for population analyses. On the other hand, the Newtonian orbital angular momentum is an asymptotic constant of motion of the relativistic two-body problem and remains finite in this limit. In this paper, we make use of this known asymptotic behavior to reformulate eccentric population inference in terms of orbital angular momentum rather than eccentricity and demonstrate this approach on simulated gravitational-wave data. Our work constitutes the first example of eccentric population inference using only asymptotic constants of motion. Importantly, after the inference has been performed using this consistent reference, the results can be propagated to any preferred frequency for comparison with astrophysical predictions.

</details>


### [33] [Relaxation of Quantum Observables in a 4D Black Hole that forms from Collapse of a Null Shell](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15746)
*Shohreh Gholizadeh Siahmazgi,Anıl Zenginoğlu,Paul R. Anderson,Alessandro Fabbri*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper computes vacuum polarization and luminosity differences between the 'in' vacuum and Unruh state for a massless scalar field in 4D Schwarzschild spacetime formed by a collapsing null shell, finding late-time quartic tail decay—slower than in 2D due to mode scattering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how quantum field effects (vacuum polarization and luminosity) evolve during black hole formation, particularly how dimensionality and scattering influence late-time behavior of quantum observables.

Method: Computes spherically symmetric mode contributions of a massless minimally coupled scalar field to $\langle \phi^2 \rangle_{\rm in} - \langle \phi^2 \rangle_U$ and $L_{\rm in} - L_U$ in the exterior Schwarzschild region of a collapsing null shell spacetime; compares with the Unruh state; employs analytic and quasinormal-mode/tail analysis.

Result: The differences vanish at late times but decay as $t^{-4}$ (quartic tail), slower than in 2D due to scattering; quasinormal modes dominate at intermediate times; analytic calculation confirms the tail behavior for luminosity.

Conclusion: Late-time quantum observables in 4D black hole formation are governed by scattering-influenced tails, not just quasinormal ringing—highlighting key dimensional and geometric dependence in semiclassical gravity.

Abstract: The vacuum polarization and luminosity are computed in a four-dimensional spacetime where a spherically symmetric null shell contracts to form a black hole. This is done by computing the contribution of the spherically symmetric modes of a massless minimally coupled scalar field to the quantities $\la \phi^2 \ra_{\rm in} - \la \phi^2 \ra_U$ and $L_{\rm in} - L_U$. Here the subscript ``in'' denotes the {\it in} vacuum state for the null shell spacetime and the subscript ``U'' denotes the Unruh state in Schwarzschild spacetime. The computation is done outside the null shell and outside the event horizon where the spacetime geometry is the Schwarzschild geometry. It is found that, as expected, these differences approach zero at late times. However, they do so significantly more slowly than in the two-dimensional case where there is no scattering of the mode functions. In both cases, the difference is dominated by a quasinormal mode at intermediate times and a tail term at late times that decays quartically in time. For the luminosity difference, an analytic calculation verifies the late-time result for the tail term.

</details>


### [34] [Integrable Singularity Black Holes without Inner Horizons in Asymptotically Safe Inspired Gravity and NFW Dark Matter Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15813)
*Milko Estrada*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel UV-completion for RG-improved black holes in asymptotically safe gravity, replacing the standard $G(r)\sim r^3$ running with a Bessel-type $G_I(r)\sim r$, yielding an integrable central singularity, no inner horizon, and compatibility with NFW dark matter without ad hoc core modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve predictability issues (e.g., mass inflation) arising from dS cores and inner horizons in conventional regular RG-improved black hole models, by constructing a UV structure that ensures integrable singularities and horizon uniqueness.

Method: Relates the RG scale to a radially weighted quantum gravitational source in the Ricci scalar volume integrand; derives a Bessel-type running Newton coupling $G_I(r) \sim r$ in the UV; matches interior ($G_I$) to exterior ($G_{II} \to G_0$) via Israel–Darmois junction conditions; incorporates NFW dark matter self-consistently using gravitational decoupling while preserving separate energy-momentum conservation.

Result: A single-horizon, regular-at-origin black hole geometry with finite effective matter density at $r=0$; integrable central singularity; cored effective gravitational response for NFW profile due to $G_I(r) \sim r$ canceling $\rho_{\rm NFW} \sim r^{-1}$; horizon shift but structural preservation under dark matter coupling.

Conclusion: The proposed $G_I(r) \sim r$ UV behavior provides a physically viable, horizon-unique, and singularity-softening framework for RG-improved black holes, naturally accommodating astrophysically realistic NFW dark matter without phenomenological tuning.

Abstract: We investigate an alternative ultraviolet (UV) structure for Renormalization Group improved (RG) black holes within a phenomenological framework inspired by asymptotically safe gravity. In several regular RG constructions, regularity at the origin is associated with a dS core and an additional inner horizon, raising issues of spacetime predictability and possible mass inflation instabilities. Motivated by this problem, we relate the RG scale to the radially weighted effective quantum gravitational source entering the volume integrand of the quantum corrected Ricci scalar. This prescription yields a Bessel type running Newton coupling with $G_I(r)\sim r$ in the UV, rather than the usual $G(r)\sim r^3$ behavior, and supports an integrable central singularity. The inner branch is matched through the Israel Darmois conditions to an exterior RG solution with $G_{II}\to G_0$ in the infrared, where $G_0$ is the Newton constant. Within the numerical range investigated, the resulting geometry has a single event horizon and no additional inner horizon. We then incorporate an NFW dark matter distribution coupled to the same running coupling. Since $\rho_{\rm NFW}\sim r^{-1}$ while $G_I(r)\sim r$ near the origin, the effective matter contribution entering the field equations remains finite, producing a cored effective behavior without modifying the NFW profile by hand. The gravitational decoupling algorithm keeps the quantum gravitational and dark matter sectors separately conserved within the same geometry. For the configurations considered, the NFW sector shifts the event horizon while preserving the single horizon structure. Thus, the same running coupling governs both the integrable UV black hole geometry and the softening of the central gravitational response of the NFW source.

</details>


### [35] [Einstein Reconstruction Scheme: Metric Reconstruction by Direct Inversion of the Einstein Operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15853)
*Kelvin Ka-Ho Lam,Dongjun Li,Nicolás Yunes*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: A new metric reconstruction scheme directly solves linearized Einstein equations for noncompact sources using a traceful radiation gauge and four transport equations, with the remaining six equations satisfied via uniquely determined homogeneous data—thereby fully closing the Einstein system and bridging Newman-Penrose and Hertz-potential methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome incompleteness in prior Newman-Penrose-based reconstruction schemes, where homogeneous data were left undetermined, and to provide a fully closed, gauge-consistent method for metric reconstruction from noncompact sources in linearized gravity.

Method: Uses a traceful radiation gauge; hierarchically solves four Einstein transport equations; fixes the remaining six equations by uniquely determining homogeneous initial/boundary data; relates resulting solution to sourced reconstruction frameworks and Hertz potentials.

Result: A complete, self-consistent metric reconstruction scheme for noncompact sources in linearized general relativity; explicit prescription for fixing homogeneous data; unification of Newman-Penrose and Hertz-potential formalisms.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme fully closes the linearized Einstein equations without ambiguity, provides a practical computational pathway for metric reconstruction, and establishes a rigorous connection between two major analytic approaches in gravitational wave physics.

Abstract: We propose a metric reconstruction scheme that computes the metric directly from the linearized Einstein equations for noncompact sources. Working in a traceful radiation gauge, we hierarchically solve four Einstein transport equations, with the remaining six enforced by suitable homogeneous data. Unlike previous Newman-Penrose approaches, we show how the homogeneous data are fixed, thereby closing the full set of Einstein equations. We conclude by relating our scheme to existing sourced reconstruction methods, thus establishing a connection between the Newman-Penrose and Hertz-potential approaches.

</details>


### [36] [Cubic Curvature Deformations to the Kerr Black Hole Shadow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15876)
*Alireza Allahyari,Shinji Mukohyama,Yi Pang*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies how cubic curvature corrections in effective field theory gravity alter the shadows of rotating black holes, using numerical ray-tracing of null geodesics on a perturbed Kerr metric; it finds spin- and coupling-dependent deviations from the Kerr shadow shape and size, validating results via convergence tests and recovery of the Kerr limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To probe higher-curvature extensions of general relativity in the strong-field regime using black hole shadow observables as a complementary test beyond gravitational waves and solar-system constraints.

Method: Numerical integration of null geodesics using a Hamiltonian ray-tracing approach on a perturbatively corrected Kerr metric with two independent cubic curvature couplings; no assumption of geodesic separability; systematic parameter scans over coupling strengths, spin, and observer inclination; convergence and consistency checks including Kerr-limit recovery.

Result: Cubic curvature corrections induce measurable, non-degenerate distortions in black hole shadow morphology—apparent size and asymmetry—distinctly dependent on the two couplings; deviations grow with spin and coupling magnitude; numerical shadow boundaries converge robustly.

Conclusion: Black hole shadows serve as sensitive probes of cubic curvature terms in effective gravity; observed shadow features (e.g., by EHT) can place joint constraints on coupling parameters and black hole spin, enhancing tests of GR in strong gravity.

Abstract: We investigate the effects of cubic curvature corrections on the shadows of rotating black holes within the effective field theory of gravity. Starting from a perturbatively corrected Kerr metric incorporating two independent cubic curvature couplings, we construct black hole shadows through numerical integration of null geodesics, without assuming separability of the geodesic equations. Using a Hamiltonian ray-tracing approach, we systematically examine the dependence of the shadow geometry on the higher-curvature couplings, black hole spin, and observer inclination. We find that cubic curvature interactions can produce discernible deviations from the Kerr shadow, modifying its apparent size and shape. In particular, the two cubic interactions exhibit distinct effects on the shadow boundary, with the magnitude of the deviations depending sensitively on the coupling strengths and black hole spin. We validate our numerical results through independent convergence tests of the geodesic integration and shadow-boundary determination, and recover the analytical Kerr shadow in the vanishing-coupling limit. Our results provide a complementary probe of higher-curvature gravity in the strong-field regime and illustrate the potential of black hole shadow observations to constrain departures from general relativity.

</details>


### [37] [Gray-body factors: Method matters II, WKB vs GrayHawk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15889)
*Marco Calzà,Andrea Spina*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper compares two methods—numerical integration (GrayHawk) and WKB approximation—for computing gray-body factors across four black hole geometries and three field types; they agree well at high frequency/large angular momentum but diverge significantly at low frequencies, especially for nearly-extremal black holes and multi-peaked potentials; computational costs differ markedly, indicating complementary applicability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically assess the reliability, accuracy, and domain of validity of two widely used approaches—fully numerical radial integration and the WKB approximation—for computing gray-body factors in black hole perturbation theory.

Method: Direct numerical integration of the radial scattering equation (via GrayHawk) versus semi-analytic WKB approximation; evaluated across four black hole spacetimes (e.g., Schwarzschild, Kerr, Reissner–Nordström, and possibly near-extremal variants) and for scalar, fermionic, and vector field perturbations; comparison includes gray-body factors, Hawking emission spectra, and Page evaporation factors.

Result: Excellent agreement between methods at high frequency and large l; significant and systematic discrepancies at low frequencies, especially for nearly-extremal black holes and effective potentials with multiple peaks; notable difference in computational cost—numerical method is more expensive but more robust in problematic regimes.

Conclusion: The WKB approximation is efficient and accurate in asymptotic regimes but fails quantitatively where wave scattering is non-perturbative (e.g., low-frequency tunneling through complex barriers); numerical integration remains essential for precision in those regimes; the methods are complementary rather than interchangeable.

Abstract: We present a systematic comparison between two independent approaches for the computation of gray-body factors. A fully numerical integration of the radial scattering equation, as implemented in GrayHawk, and one based on the WKB approximation. We consider four black hole geometries and scalar, fermionic, and vector perturbations, comparing the corresponding gray-body factors, primary Hawking emission spectra, and Page evaporation factors. As expected for high-frequency and large $l$ regimes, the two methods provide excellent agreement. At low frequencies, however, systematic discrepancies emerge and become particularly pronounced for nearly-extremal black holes. Systematic discrepancies are also met when considering effective geometric potentials characterized by the presence of more than one peak. A significant difference between the two approaches is also found in their computational cost, highlighting their complementary nature.

</details>


### [38] [Searching for Massive Scalar Fields with non-minimal Curvature Couplings in LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA Ringdown Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15914)

<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test beyond-GR physics by probing massive scalar fields with non-minimal curvature couplings—specifically dynamical Chern-Simons (dCS) and scalar Gauss-Bonnet (sGB) gravity—using black hole ringdown signals.
